# ROUND 2/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Understanding the mission:**
- Before switch time: penalized for stationary robots → want robots MOVING
- After switch time: penalized for moving robots → want robots STOPPED
- Switch time is random between 400-600 steps, total run is 1200 steps
- At switch time, a GREEN wall lights up → robots can detect green (color 2) with camera condition C=7, l=2

**Key insight:** The optimal strategy is:
1. Move before switch (exploration/repulsion)
2. Stop after switch (when green wall appears)
3. Green wall detection (C=7, l=2) is the signal to transition to stop

**Analysis of champion (score 11656):**
- State 0: Repulsion (moving), multiple transitions including neighbor counts and gray floor
- State 1: Stop, transitions back to repulsion on neighbor count or gray floor
- This works reasonably well but doesn't explicitly use the green wall signal

**Strategy for round 2:**
1. **Survival**: Keep champion exactly
2. **Exploitation**: Variants of champion with green detection added, tweaked probabilities
3. **Exploration**: 2-state FSMs with explicit green detection to switch from moving to stopped

The green wall (color 2) appearing is the key signal. After it appears, robots should stop. Before it appears, robots should move.

Let me design machines that:
- Start in a moving state (exploration/repulsion)
- Transition to stop when green is detected (C=7, l=2)
- Have fallback transitions

FSM candidates:

1. Champion (exact copy)
2. Champion variant: add green detection to state 0
3. 2-state: repulsion → stop on green detection
4. 2-state: exploration → stop on green detection  
5. 3-state: repulsion → stop on green, with neighbor-based fallback
6. Variant with lower transition probabilities for green
7. Champion variant with tweaked parameters
8. 2-state: attraction then stop
9. 3-state more complex
10. Exploration variants

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations from round 2:**

The champion (score 7170) is remarkably simple:
- State 0: Attraction (moving toward neighbors), with transition to Stop when GREEN is seen (C=7, l=2, p=0.95), and a tiny random fallback (p=0.002)
- State 1: Stop, with tiny probability of returning to attraction (p=0.001)

The core insight is confirmed: **detect green wall → stop**. The attraction behavior keeps robots moving before the switch, and the green detection triggers the stop.

**Gap analysis:**
- Top 3 (7170, 7709, 8215) use the simple 2-state pattern: [moving behavior] → stop on green
- Rank 4-10 (10260-11630) have extra transitions that seem to hurt
- The simplicity of the champion is key

**Strategy:**
1. Keep champion exactly
2. Exploit: vary attraction gain (2.0-4.5), green detection probability (0.9-0.99), stop-return probability
3. Explore: different movement behaviors (repulsion with lower gain, different rwm values)
4. Try: higher green detection probability, near-zero return probability from stop
5. Try: 3-state with an intermediate state

The champion uses att=3.0, p_green=0.95, p_return=0.002, p_stop_return=0.001. Let me vary these systematically.

Key parameters to tune:
- Attraction gain: 1.5, 2.0, 2.5, 3.0, 3.5, 4.0, 4.5
- Green detection prob: 0.85, 0.90, 0.95, 0.99
- Return probability from stop: 0.0005, 0.001, 0.002

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations from round 3:**

The pattern is very clear now. The best machines all share this topology:
- State 0: Moving behavior, transition to Stop when green seen (C=7, l=2), tiny random fallback
- State 1: Stop, tiny probability of returning to moving

**Parameter analysis:**
- att=4.0 and att=4.5 tied at 7406 (better than champion's 7170 was in round 2, but champion still best overall)
- att=3.0, p_green=0.9 scored 7432 (close to champion)
- p_green=0.99 scored worse (8316) - too slow to detect green
- Lower attraction gains (1.5-2.5) scored worse
- 3-state machines score much worse - complexity hurts
- rwm=10 (7802) better than rwm=30 (8725) - slow turns help

**What to try:**
1. Champion (exact copy, att=3.0, p=0.95, p_fallback=0.002)
2. att=4.0 variants (tied for best this round)
3. att=4.5 variants
4. Try att=5.0 (maximum)
5. Try slightly different p_green values (0.92, 0.95, 0.97)
6. Try different fallback probabilities (0.001, 0.002, 0.003)
7. Try p_stop_return=0.0 effectively (very tiny like 0.0001)
8. Try repulsion with gain 4.0-5.0
9. Try rwm=5 (very slow turning)
10. GoToColor green (behavior 8, clr=2) - move toward green wall then stop?

The champion score is 7170 from round 1. The best this round was 7406. Let me focus on att=3.5-5.0 with p_green around 0.93-0.97.

Also worth trying: GoToColor (B=8) toward green wall - this would actively move robots toward the green wall before stopping, which might cluster them better. But wait - after switch they need to STOP, so moving toward green then stopping could work.

Actually, let me reconsider: maybe the issue is that with attraction, robots cluster together and when green appears, they all stop. The stopping is what matters. Let me try att=4.0 with varied p values.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key breakthrough in round 4:**

The new champion uses **GoToColor green (B=8, clr=2)** - robots actively move toward the green wall! This is brilliant because:
- Before switch: no green wall → robots wander (GoToColor with no target visible still moves)
- After switch: green wall appears → robots rush toward it AND detect green → stop

Score 5975 vs 7170 before - a big improvement.

**Parameter analysis:**
- GoToColor green (B=8, clr=2): 5975 (best!)
- att=4, p_fallback=0.003, p_stop=0.0005: 6078 (close second)
- att=5: 6180
- att=4, p=0.97: 6475
- att=4, p=0.95: 6564

**What to exploit around the champion:**
- GoToColor green with varied p_green (0.90, 0.93, 0.95, 0.97, 0.99)
- GoToColor green with varied fallback probability
- GoToColor green with different stop-return probabilities
- GoToColor away from non-green colors while looking for green

**What to explore:**
- GoToColor green + GoToColor green in stop (to cluster near wall)
- GoToColor green then attraction (after seeing green, attract to stay near wall)
- Try GoAwayColor non-green before switch (to keep moving)

The champion: GoToColor green (clr=2), p_green=0.95, p_fallback=0.002, p_stop_return=0.001

## Reasoning

**Champion analysis (score 5603):**
- State 0: GoToColor green (B=8, clr=2), transition to Stop when green seen (p=0.95), tiny fallback (p=0.002)
- State 1: Stop, very tiny return probability (p=0.0005)

Key finding: **p_stop_return=0.0005 is better than 0.001**. The stop state should be almost perfectly absorbing.

**Parameter patterns from round 5:**
- p_stop_return: 0.0005 wins (5603 vs 5816 for 0.001 variant)
- p_fallback (random transition from state 0): 0.002 seems best (0.001 gave 5816, 0.003 gave 6576, 0.004 gave 6932)
- p_green: 0.95 seems good (0.99 gave 6133, 0.97 gave 6128, 0.93 gave 6122, 0.90 gave 6122) - all close
- Extra transitions hurt (rank 14 with neighbor condition: 10053!)

**What to try:**
1. Champion (exact)
2. Vary p_fallback: 0.0005, 0.001, 0.0015, 0.002, 0.0025
3. Vary p_stop_return: 0.0001, 0.0002, 0.0003, 0.0005
4. Vary p_green: 0.95, 0.97 combined with better stop return
5. Try p_fallback=0.001, p_stop=0.0005 (rank 2 was close)
6. Try virtually zero fallback from state 0 (0.0001) - robots just go to green and stop
7. Explore: GoAwayColor (non-green) before switch

The key insight: lower p_stop_return is better (robots stay stopped longer). Lower p_fallback from state 0 might also help (less accidental stopping before green appears). But too low p_fallback could be bad if robots are truly stuck.

Actually wait - the fallback p=0.002 from state 0 causes robots to transition to Stop even before green appears. That's bad! They should only stop AFTER green appears. So lower p_fallback should be better... but rank 2 (p_fallback=0.001) scored 5816, while champion (p_fallback=0.002) scored 5603. That's counterintuitive.

Maybe the fallback helps because some robots can't see green (blocked by walls/other robots) and the random stop helps coordinate?

Let me try very low p_fallback values combined with very low p_stop_return.

## Reasoning

**Champion (score 5379):**
- GoToColor green (B=8, clr=2)
- p_green=0.95, p_fallback=0.002, p_stop_return=0.0001

**Clear trends from round 6:**
- p_stop_return: 0.0001 wins (5379), 0.0002 (5526), 0.0003 (5724), 0.0005 (5989) - **lower is better, clear trend**
- p_fallback from state 0: 0.002 best with 0.0001 stop return; 0.001 also good (5611 with 0.0002)
- p_green=0.95 still competitive; 0.99/0.97 slightly worse when combined with 0.0005 stop return

**What to try:**
1. Try p_stop_return even lower: 0.00005, 0.00001, 0.0 (but must be >0 for valid probability... actually 0.0 should be valid)
2. Try p_fallback=0.001 or 0.0015 with p_stop_return=0.0001
3. Try p_fallback=0.002 with p_stop_return=0.00005
4. Try p_green variations with p_stop_return=0.0001
5. Explore: can we have p_stop_return=0.0? Let's try 0.0 (absorbing stop state)

The trend suggests p_stop_return→0 helps. The stop state becoming fully absorbing makes sense: once robots stop after green appears, they should NEVER restart. The only cost is pre-switch accidental stops from p_fallback=0.002 (expected ~2.4 stops per robot in 1200 steps... actually 0.002 × 1200 = 2.4 expected transitions to stop before green). But if stop is absorbing, robots who accidentally stop before green are penalized heavily for moving phase.

Wait - if stop is absorbing with p=0.0, robots who accidentally stop before switch stay stopped (good for after-switch score). But the penalty before switch for being stopped accumulates. With p_fallback=0.002, expected time to first accidental stop is 500 steps, which is right around the switch time!

So the balance is: p_fallback controls when robots stop (should be around switch time), and p_stop_return=0 makes stop permanent.

Let me try p_fallback values around 0.001-0.003 with p_stop_return=0.00005 and 0.0.

Also try: p_stop_return=0.0 (fully absorbing stop).

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Champion (score 5053):**
- GoToColor green, p_green=0.95, p_fallback=0.002, p_stop_return=0.0 (fully absorbing!)

**Key findings:**
- p_stop_return=0.0 wins decisively (5053 vs 5066 for 0.0001)
- Stop state should be completely absorbing
- p_fallback=0.002 seems optimal (0.0015 gave 5066, 0.001 gave 5368, 0.0025 gave 5454, 0.003 gave 5648)
- p_green=0.95 with p_stop=0: best
- p_green=0.99 with p_stop=0.0001: 5249
- p_green=0.97 with p_stop=0.0001: 5250

**What to try now:**
The champion is very well-tuned. Now let me:
1. Keep champion exactly
2. Try p_fallback variations around 0.002 with p_stop=0.0: 0.0010, 0.0015, 0.0018, 0.0020, 0.0022, 0.0025
3. Try p_green variations with p_stop=0.0: 0.90, 0.93, 0.97, 0.99
4. Try p_fallback=0.0 (no fallback at all) with p_stop=0.0 - robots ONLY stop when they see green

The optimal p_fallback should balance: too low → robots that can't see green never stop; too high → robots stop too early before switch. With switch at ~500 steps and 1200 total:
- Pre-switch penalty: p_fallback × steps_before_switch × 20 robots
- Post-switch penalty: (1-p_green) × steps_after_switch × robots_not_stopped

The fallback also helps robots that might be facing away from green and can't detect it.

Let me also try: p_fallback=0.0 (pure green detection only) - if robots always face green (GoToColor), they should always see it when it appears.

## Reasoning

**Champion (score 5053):** GoToColor green, p_green=0.95, p_fallback=0.002, p_stop_return=0.0

**Round 8 analysis:**
- Best this round: p_fallback=0.0015 with p_stop=0.0 (scores 5235, 5235) - slightly worse than champion
- p_fallback=0.002 with p_stop=0.0 scored 5352 this round (vs 5053 champion - stochastic variation)
- p_fallback=0.0 (no fallback) scored 7429 - terrible! Robots with no fallback get stuck moving forever if they can't see green
- p_fallback trend: 0.001 (5258) < 0.0015 (5235) < 0.0018 (5245) < 0.002 (5352) < 0.0022 (5514) < 0.0025 (5630) < 0.003 (5791)

So p_fallback=0.0015 seems to be emerging as slightly better than 0.002 with p_stop=0.0.

**Key insight:** The champion score of 5053 was achieved with p_fallback=0.002, p_stop=0.0. This round similar configs scored ~5235-5352 (stochastic). The optimal p_fallback is around 0.0015-0.002.

**Strategy for round 9:**
1. Keep champion exactly
2. Fine-tune p_fallback: 0.0012, 0.0013, 0.0014, 0.0015, 0.0016, 0.0017, 0.0018, 0.0019, 0.002
3. Fine-tune p_green: 0.95, 0.97 combined with best p_fallback range
4. The p_stop=0.0 is confirmed best

The sweet spot for p_fallback seems to be 0.0015-0.002. Let me sample densely in this range.

- p_fallback=0.0019 wins (4869)
- p_fallback=0.0015 (4876/4877/4877/4881) - very close, nearly tied
- p_fallback=0.0016 (4903), 0.0017 (4967), 0.002 (4975), 0.0012 (4977), 0.0014 (5012), 0.0013 (5072)
- p_green doesn't matter much: 0.93-0.99 all scored ~4876-4881 with p_fallback=0.0015

**Key insight:** The scores are very close in the range 0.0015-0.002. The optimal p_fallback is around 0.0015-0.0019. The p_green value seems largely irrelevant (scores within noise range for 0.93-0.99).

3. Since p_green is irrelevant, fix at 0.95 and vary p_fallback densely

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
